The paper "Towards Operationalizing Heterogeneous Data Discovery" addresses the challenge of querying and analyzing multi-modal data lakes, which are common in modern enterprises but often more complex than the structured-data-only scenarios typically addressed in existing research. The authors propose an end-to-end system with a declarative query interface built upon a set of multi-modal operators that extend relational operations with AI-composed ones.  They formally define essential steps like data discovery, query planning, processing, and result aggregation. The paper identifies research challenges and discusses opportunities for optimization using Large Language Models (LLMs), and it demonstrates preliminary attempts in benchmarking. The paper envisions a unified query engine that can support various kinds of queries, including SQL queries, model inference, and prompt based questions, on data with various formats.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** While the individual components discussed are not entirely novel (e.g., data discovery, multi-modal querying, LLM integration), the *combination* of these elements into a comprehensive, declarative framework for multi-modal data lakes has some degree of novelty. Previous research tends to focus on specific aspects, such as question answering with LLMs or data discovery for structured data, rather than providing a holistic view. The authors do highlight the differences with previous works. However, they do not demonstrate how the multi-modal operators defined extend the relational model, e.g. they could give a formal definition. Also, the paper lacks a concrete implementation and experimental evaluation of the entire proposed system. So, while the idea itself is novel, its realization is at a very early stage.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper lies in addressing a real-world problem: the increasing complexity and heterogeneity of enterprise data. The proposed framework, if successfully implemented, could significantly improve the efficiency and usability of data lakes, enabling organizations to extract more value from their diverse data assets. The integration of LLMs is also a promising avenue for enhancing data discovery and query processing. The framework also brings up more opportunities to incorporate more semantics with language models. However, the paper primarily presents a vision and lacks empirical evidence to demonstrate its practical benefits. A working prototype and comparison with existing approaches would greatly strengthen the paper's impact.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper clearly articulates the problem of querying heterogeneous data lakes.
    *   The concept of multi-modal operators provides a structured way to handle different data types.
    *   The identification of research challenges is valuable for guiding future work.
    *   The integration of LLMs is a timely and relevant approach.
    *   The inclusion of a benchmark for data discovery is a good starting point for evaluating the framework.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The paper is primarily conceptual; it lacks a concrete implementation and detailed evaluation.
    *   The multi-modal operators are defined at a high level. More detail is needed on how they are implemented and optimized.
    *   The discussion of query planning and optimization is relatively superficial.
    *   The benchmark is limited in scope and doesn't fully assess the end-to-end performance of the proposed system.
    *   The paper needs more concrete examples of how the system would handle different types of queries and data sources.
    *   No formal definition of how operators defined are extending relational model
    *   Experimental Evaluation is not quite complete

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in data management and AI. It highlights the need for more sophisticated techniques for querying and analyzing heterogeneous data, and it provides a roadmap for developing such techniques. If followed up with a strong implementation and empirical evaluation, this work could become a foundational contribution in the field. However, its current level of conceptual development and lack of empirical validation limit its immediate impact.

**Justification:** The paper presents a promising vision for operationalizing heterogeneous data discovery, but it is still in the early stages of development. The core idea of a declarative framework with multi-modal operators is novel, but the lack of concrete implementation and evaluation makes it difficult to assess its true potential. The identified research challenges are valuable, but the paper needs to provide more specific solutions and demonstrate their effectiveness in practice.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.DB
- **Score**: 6/10

### Aligned Better, Listen Better for Audio-Visual Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02061v1)
- **Authors**: Yuxin Guo, Shuailei Ma, Shijie Ma, Xiaoyi Bao, Chen-Wei Xie, Kecheng Zheng, Tingyu Weng, Siyang Sun, Yun Zheng, Wei Zou
- **Abstract**: Audio is essential for multimodal video understanding. On the one hand, video inherently contains audio, which supplies complementary information to vision. Besides, video large language models (Video-LLMs) can encounter many audio-centric settings. However, existing Video-LLMs and Audio-Visual Large Language Models (AV-LLMs) exhibit deficiencies in exploiting audio information, leading to weak understanding and hallucinations. To solve the issues, we delve into the model architecture and dataset. (1) From the architectural perspective, we propose a fine-grained AV-LLM, namely Dolphin. The concurrent alignment of audio and visual modalities in both temporal and spatial dimensions ensures a comprehensive and accurate understanding of videos. Specifically, we devise an audio-visual multi-scale adapter for multi-scale information aggregation, which achieves spatial alignment. For temporal alignment, we propose audio-visual interleaved merging. (2) From the dataset perspective, we curate an audio-visual caption and instruction-tuning dataset, called AVU. It comprises 5.2 million diverse, open-ended data tuples (video, audio, question, answer) and introduces a novel data partitioning strategy. Extensive experiments show our model not only achieves remarkable performance in audio-visual understanding, but also mitigates potential hallucinations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Dolphin, a fine-grained Audio-Visual Large Language Model (AV-LLM) designed to improve audio understanding in multimodal video understanding.  Current AV-LLMs often neglect or hallucinate audio information. To address this, the authors propose architectural improvements and a new dataset. The architectural improvements in Dolphin include a concurrent alignment of audio and visual modalities through an audio-visual multi-scale adapter for spatial alignment and audio-visual interleaved merging for temporal alignment. The authors also introduce AVU, a large-scale audio-visual captioning and instruction-tuning dataset with 5.2 million diverse data tuples, partitioned based on audio-visual consistency. Experiments demonstrate that Dolphin achieves enhanced audio-visual understanding and reduced hallucinations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty of the paper lies in the combination of architectural enhancements *and* the creation of a large, specifically designed dataset. While multi-scale feature fusion and interleaved merging are not entirely new concepts in multimodal learning, their specific adaptation and implementation within the AV-LLM context are noteworthy. The AVU dataset, with its focus on audio-visual consistency and diversity, is a significant contribution, especially considering the scarcity of such resources. However, the individual architectural components (multi-scale adapters, attention mechanisms, etc.) build upon existing research. The key novelty is their *combination* for audio-visual alignment and the specific data-driven strategy used to address the model's bias towards visual information.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial limitation of existing AV-LLMs: their poor audio understanding and tendency to hallucinate. This limitation is particularly relevant in audio-centric applications and for comprehensive video understanding, where audio plays a complementary or essential role. The improved performance of Dolphin, as evidenced by the experiments, demonstrates the potential of the proposed approach. The AVU dataset provides a valuable resource for the research community, enabling further exploration of audio-visual understanding. The ablation studies are crucial in justifying the contributions of the key components. The experiments on unimodal tasks further validate the claim that Dolphin has truly learned to exploit the information from both complementary modalities, rather than relying primarily on vision.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper identifies a clear and important problem.
    *   The proposed architectural solutions and the dataset are well-motivated and designed.
    *   The experimental results convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of Dolphin.
    *   The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contributions of individual components.
    *   The qualitative results in Figure 7 also show that it can better leverage and understand videos compared to the prior work.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the individual components are combined novelly, they build on existing techniques. The paper would be strengthened by demonstrating a specific innovation in one or more of these components for AV-LLMs.
    *   The architectural details could be more detailed. For example, exact sizes, dimensions, and hyperparameter choices are omitted.
    *   The reliance on Vicuna as the LLM might limit the overall performance potential. Evaluating Dolphin with a more recent or larger LLM would further showcase the benefits of their approach.
    *   While the paper shows that the model reduces hallucination, it may be improved by introducing additional losses to more effectively avoid AV-related hallucinations.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more capable and reliable AV-LLMs. The AVU dataset is likely to become a valuable benchmark for evaluating audio-visual understanding. The architectural insights provided by the paper can guide future research in multimodal learning.

**Justification for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of 7.5/10.

*   The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of audio-visual learning by addressing a critical limitation of existing AV-LLMs. The combination of architectural enhancements and the creation of a new, large-scale dataset represents a significant step forward. The experimental results convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach. However, the individual architectural components build upon existing research, and the paper could benefit from more detailed implementation details and evaluation with a larger LLM. Moreover, while demonstrating a reduction in hallucination, additional techniques to mitigate potential hallucinations could be further explored. Therefore, while the paper is impactful and innovative, it is not quite ground-breaking enough to warrant a higher score.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### From Text to Graph: Leveraging Graph Neural Networks for Enhanced Explainability in NLP
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02064v1)
- **Authors**: Fabio Yáñez-Romero, Andrés Montoyo, Armando Suárez, Yoan Gutiérrez, Ruslan Mitkov
- **Abstract**: Researchers have relegated natural language processing tasks to Transformer-type models, particularly generative models, because these models exhibit high versatility when performing generation and classification tasks. As the size of these models increases, they achieve outstanding results. Given their widespread use, many explainability techniques are developed based on these models. However, this process becomes computationally expensive due to the large size of the models. Additionally, transformers interpret input information through tokens that fragment input words into sequences lacking inherent semantic meaning, complicating the explanation of the model from the very beginning. This study proposes a novel methodology to achieve explainability in natural language processing tasks by automatically converting sentences into graphs and maintaining semantics through nodes and relations that express fundamental linguistic concepts. It also allows the subsequent exploitation of this knowledge in subsequent tasks, making it possible to obtain trends and understand how the model associates the different elements inside the text with the explained task. The experiments delivered promising results in determining the most critical components within the text structure for a given classification.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel methodology for explainability in natural language processing (NLP) tasks.  Instead of relying on inherently opaque Transformer models and their tokenization methods, it advocates for converting sentences into graphs that explicitly represent semantic relationships using linguistic concepts (dependency or constituency trees).  These graphs are then processed by Graph Neural Networks (GNNs), and explainability is achieved using post-hoc explainability methods like SubgraphX, optimized with AutoGOAL. The method extracts the most critical subgraphs and performs semantic and structural analysis on them to understand how the model associates text elements with the classification task. The authors perform experiments on topic classification and sentiment analysis datasets to demonstrate the feasibility of their approach and provide information of the importance of some sentences to get the expected result.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its systematic combination of techniques to achieve explainability in NLP, moving away from the standard Transformer-centric approach. Specifically, the novel parts include the automated process of the following:

1.  Converting sentences into linguistic graphs (dependency or constituency trees). This isn't entirely novel in itself, but it is done with a clear explainability objective.
2.  Using these graphs as input to a GNN trained to replicate the classification behavior of a pre-trained language model.
3.  Employing SubgraphX, an explainability module, and *crucially* automating hyperparameter optimization with AutoGOAL.
4.  Performing both semantic and structural analysis of the extracted subgraphs to gain a deeper understanding of the model's decisions.

While each component has been explored in isolation before, the integrated pipeline and the focus on semantic interpretability through graph structures, offer a fresh perspective. Also, the combination of constituency parsing + GNN distillation + SubgraphX + AutoGOAL hasn't, to my knowledge, been done before, and is a good way to get interpretable data from complex models. The use of the tree representation is a constraint of the original space based on explicitly stated language rules and a space with less knowledge that can avoid confusion within the model by using much less data during training.

**Significance:** The significance stems from the potential to provide more interpretable and traceable explanations for NLP models. Transformers are powerful but notoriously hard to understand. This paper offers a viable alternative pathway that leverages the inherent explainability of graph structures and GNNs. The semantic transparency of graph nodes (representing words or linguistic phrases) is also a major advantage compared to token-based explanations in Transformers. Also, the models are much smaller than transformer-based models, leading to potential for faster and cheaper implementation. The combination with AutoGOAL is great because, in general, explainability modules are extremely dependent on hyperparameter settings that are usually only properly set through extensive experimentation.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear methodology:** The paper clearly outlines each step of the proposed methodology, making it easy to follow and potentially replicate.
*   **Semantic interpretability:**  The use of graph structures based on linguistic concepts preserves semantic meaning, facilitating human understanding of the model's reasoning.
*   **Automated optimization:** The use of AutoGOAL for hyperparameter optimization of the explainability module enhances the reliability and significance of the results.
*   **Empirical validation:** The experiments on topic classification and sentiment analysis demonstrate the feasibility of the approach.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited scope:** The experiments are conducted on relatively simple datasets (AG News, SST-2). The performance and scalability of the approach on more complex, real-world datasets need to be investigated.
*   **GNN mimicking the LM:** The approach depends on the GNN's ability to closely mimic the LM's classification behavior. Any significant discrepancy could limit the validity of the explanations. There needs to be an analysis of what kind of tasks or LM's are more suitable to be explained using this methodology.
*   **Constituency parsing quality:** The constituency parsing is also extremely important, and bad constituency parsers might skew the results or just make the graphs harder to handle computationally.
*   **Limited structural analysis:** The discussion of the structural analysis is quite superficial. More in-depth exploration of graph properties and their correlation with classification outcomes would strengthen the paper.
*   **Generalizability of the subgraphs found is unknown.** The usefulness of this analysis is linked to identifying similar subgraphs that influence in the same manner results on other examples, and that is something that is not covered in the paper.

**Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by:

*   Providing a viable alternative to Transformer-centric explainability techniques.
*   Encouraging the use of structured knowledge representations (graphs) for NLP tasks.
*   Promoting the development of more interpretable and traceable NLP models.

**Justification of Score:**

While the paper presents a promising and potentially impactful methodology, its limitations in scope and depth of analysis warrant a slightly more conservative score. The clarity of the methodology, the focus on semantic interpretability, and the automation of explainability optimization are all strong points. However, the limited experimental validation and relatively shallow structural analysis hold it back from achieving a higher score. Therefore:

**Score: 6.5**

This reflects the paper's solid contribution to explainable NLP, balanced by the need for further research to fully assess its applicability and generalizability. The paper does a good job of presenting the methodology, but the potential of it is just partially shown due to the experimental limitations described above.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Evolving Security in LLMs: A Study of Jailbreak Attacks and Defenses
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02080v1)
- **Authors**: Zhengchun Shang, Wenlan Wei
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly popular, powering a wide range of applications. Their widespread use has sparked concerns, especially through jailbreak attacks that bypass safety measures to produce harmful content. In this paper, we present a comprehensive security analysis of large language models (LLMs), addressing critical research questions on the evolution and determinants of model safety. Specifically, we begin by identifying the most effective techniques for detecting jailbreak attacks. Next, we investigate whether newer versions of LLMs offer improved security compared to their predecessors. We also assess the impact of model size on overall security and explore the potential benefits of integrating multiple defense strategies to enhance model robustness. Our study evaluates both open-source models (e.g., LLaMA and Mistral) and closed-source systems (e.g., GPT-4) by employing four state-of-the-art attack techniques and assessing the efficacy of three new defensive approaches.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Evolving Security in LLMs: A Study of Jailbreak Attacks and Defenses" presents a comprehensive empirical investigation into the security vulnerabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) against jailbreak attacks. The authors focus on understanding the impact of model scale, architecture, and version on susceptibility to these attacks. They evaluate several state-of-the-art attack methods across various open-source (LLaMA, Mistral) and closed-source (GPT-4) LLMs.  The study also examines the effectiveness of three defense strategies: Goal Prioritization, Llama Guard, and Smooth-LLM. The paper identifies the best methods for detecting jailbreak attacks, assesses the relative vulnerability of different LLM architectures, and evaluates the performance of defenses, offering practical guidance for practitioners seeking to harden their systems. The code used in their study is planned to be publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The paper conducts a thorough and systematic evaluation of jailbreak attacks and defenses across a diverse set of LLMs.  The inclusion of both open-source and closed-source models is a significant strength.
*   **Clear Research Questions:** The study addresses important research questions related to model evolution, defense effectiveness, and the interplay of various factors affecting LLM security.
*   **Rigorous Methodology:** The authors employ a rigorous experimental methodology, including automated attack detection and quantitative metrics for evaluation (ASR, PE). The explicit focus on controlling for confounding factors like model scale and version is commendable.
*   **Practical Insights:** The paper provides actionable insights for practitioners, highlighting the relative robustness of certain architectures, the limitations of version updates, and the effectiveness of various defense strategies. The effort to assess performance overhead is also valuable.
*   **Reproducibility:** The authors explicitly state that their code will be available, enhancing the reproducibility of their work.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Defenses:** While the paper explores three defense strategies, the broader landscape of LLM security defenses is vast.  The study's conclusions might not generalize to all possible defense mechanisms. The specific methods tested may already be surpassed by newer techniques by the time of publication.
*   **Static Dataset:** The ground truth used is based on manual annotation, this approach is expensive. The ground truth dataset used for training is also static and may be of lower quality, as it's hard to keep the test set free of examples similar to those that models have been trained on.
*   **Reliance on Default Hyperparameters:** The study's decision to use default hyperparameter settings is a potential limitation. Exploring the sensitivity of LLM security to hyperparameter tuning could reveal further insights.
*   **Evolutionary Advancements in Models:** The paper does not take into account how specific evolutionary advancements in model building/training impact jailbreaking, and instead just compares the model versions. This could provide a better insight into the impact of model evolution on jailbreaking.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper's novelty lies in its comprehensive and systematic approach to evaluating LLM security. While individual aspects like specific attacks or defenses may have been previously explored, this study offers a holistic view of the interplay between model evolution, architecture, scale, and security. It significantly contributes to the growing body of knowledge on LLM vulnerabilities and provides a valuable benchmark for future research. In addition, the paper addresses and discusses many limitations in its findings, which is a large positive in terms of novelty and significance of the study.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable and important contribution to the field of LLM security. It offers a systematic empirical study that addresses key research questions and provides actionable insights. The limitations are acknowledged, and the reproducible research practice further strengthens its value. Given the comprehensiveness, rigor, and practical relevance of the study, a high score is justified, but a perfect score is not given due to the previously mentioned limitations.
Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 8/10

### Increasing happiness through conversations with artificial intelligence
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02091v1)
- **Authors**: Joseph Heffner, Chongyu Qin, Martin Chadwick, Chris Knutsen, Christopher Summerfield, Zeb Kurth-Nelson, Robb B. Rutledge
- **Abstract**: Chatbots powered by artificial intelligence (AI) have rapidly become a significant part of everyday life, with over a quarter of American adults using them multiple times per week. While these tools offer potential benefits and risks, a fundamental question remains largely unexplored: How do conversations with AI influence subjective well-being? To investigate this, we conducted a study where participants either engaged in conversations with an AI chatbot (N = 334) or wrote journal entires (N = 193) on the same randomly assigned topics and reported their momentary happiness afterward. We found that happiness after AI chatbot conversations was higher than after journaling, particularly when discussing negative topics such as depression or guilt. Leveraging large language models for sentiment analysis, we found that the AI chatbot mirrored participants' sentiment while maintaining a consistent positivity bias. When discussing negative topics, participants gradually aligned their sentiment with the AI's positivity, leading to an overall increase in happiness. We hypothesized that the history of participants' sentiment prediction errors, the difference between expected and actual emotional tone when responding to the AI chatbot, might explain this happiness effect. Using computational modeling, we find the history of these sentiment prediction errors over the course of a conversation predicts greater post-conversation happiness, demonstrating a central role of emotional expectations during dialogue. Our findings underscore the effect that AI interactions can have on human well-being.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary, critical evaluation, and novelty assessment of the provided paper based on the OCR text.

**Summary:**

This paper explores the impact of conversations with AI chatbots on subjective well-being, comparing it to journaling. The study found that AI chatbot conversations led to higher happiness levels compared to journaling, particularly when discussing negative topics like depression or guilt. This effect was attributed to the chatbot's ability to mirror the participant's sentiment while maintaining a consistent positive bias, leading participants to gradually align their sentiment with the AI's. The paper introduces a novel computational model based on sentiment prediction errors (sPE) and demonstrates that the history of these prediction errors during the conversation predicts post-conversation happiness. The authors also analyze the conversational dynamics, showing that chatbots steer conversations towards a more positive semantic space and that both the chatbot and participants contribute independently to post-conversation happiness. Finally, the paper demonstrates that this effect is predominantly driven by discussions centered around negative topics.

**Critical Evaluation and Novelty Assessment:**

This paper addresses a timely and relevant question: how do interactions with AI chatbots influence our subjective well-being? Given the increasing prevalence of these technologies, understanding their impact is crucial. While previous work has explored the use of chatbots for mental health, this paper's contribution lies in several areas:

*   **Novelty of the Question:** The paper directly addresses how AI chatbot *interactions* affect momentary subjective well-being. This is a distinct contribution compared to rating of chatbot outputs or simulations.
*   **Control Comparison:** Comparing chatbot conversations to journaling provides a solid baseline. It allows isolating the interactive element of the chatbot from simple reflection.
*   **Computational Modeling:** The introduction of the sentiment prediction error model is a strength. It provides a potential mechanism for how these conversations impact happiness. The demonstration that the model generalizes to the journaling condition strengthens the claim.
*   **Granular Analysis:** The analysis of conversational dynamics, including sentiment mirroring, positive bias, and bidirectional influence, provides valuable insights into the process of chatbot conversation.
*   **Focus on Negative Topics:** Finding that AI chatbots can be particularly beneficial for addressing negative topics is significant. It suggests a potential avenue for targeted interventions.
*   **Large Sample Size:** Using a relatively large sample enhances the study's statistical power.
*   **Reproducibility:**  The authors are using established research tools and techniques.

**However, some weaknesses need consideration:**

*   **Momentary Well-Being Only:** The study primarily focuses on momentary happiness. The long-term effects remain unexamined. It's unclear whether the observed happiness boost translates into sustained well-being improvements.
*   **General-Purpose Chatbot:** The chatbot wasn't specifically designed for mental health. While this allows generalizability, a tailored chatbot might yield even more significant results.
*   **Causation vs. Correlation:** While the sentiment prediction error model suggests a mechanism, it's challenging to establish definitive causality. Other factors might be at play.
*   **Ethical Considerations:** While the paper mentions potential risks, a more in-depth discussion of user privacy, over-dependence, and the potential for manipulation would strengthen the work.
*   **The study doesn't account for the different kinds of chatbot used by the participants.**

**Significance:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by demonstrating that conversations with AI chatbots can positively impact subjective well-being, particularly for negative emotional states. It provides a foundation for future research exploring how to design chatbots to maximize their therapeutic benefits while mitigating potential risks. The findings have implications for mental health interventions, AI design, and the broader understanding of human-AI interactions.

**Justification of Score:**

Given the novelty, significance, and methodological strengths, but also acknowledging the limitations, a score of **7** is assigned. The paper addresses an important and timely question using a well-controlled design and advanced analytical techniques. The findings are potentially valuable for understanding and improving mental well-being. However, the focus on momentary happiness and the lack of long-term follow-up studies prevent a higher score. A deeper examination of ethical concerns and alternative explanations could further strengthen the work.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### FlowDistill: Scalable Traffic Flow Prediction via Distillation from LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02094v1)
- **Authors**: Chenyang Yu, Xinpeng Xie, Yan Huang, Chenxi Qiu
- **Abstract**: Accurate traffic flow prediction is vital for optimizing urban mobility, yet it remains difficult in many cities due to complex spatio-temporal dependencies and limited high-quality data. While deep graph-based models demonstrate strong predictive power, their performance often comes at the cost of high computational overhead and substantial training data requirements, making them impractical for deployment in resource-constrained or data-scarce environments. We propose the FlowDistill, a lightweight and scalable traffic prediction framework based on knowledge distillation from large language models (LLMs). In this teacher-student setup, a fine-tuned LLM guides a compact multi-layer perceptron (MLP) student model using a novel combination of the information bottleneck principle and teacher-bounded regression loss, ensuring the distilled model retains only essential and transferable knowledge. Spatial and temporal correlations are explicitly encoded to enhance the model's generalization across diverse urban settings. Despite its simplicity, FlowDistill consistently outperforms state-of-the-art models in prediction accuracy while requiring significantly less training data, and achieving lower memory usage and inference latency, highlighting its efficiency and suitability for real-world, scalable deployment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "FlowDistill: Scalable Traffic Flow Prediction via Distillation from LLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FlowDistill, a framework for traffic flow prediction designed to be both accurate and computationally efficient, especially in scenarios with limited training data. It leverages knowledge distillation, using a fine-tuned Large Language Model (LLM) as a teacher to guide a smaller, Multi-Layer Perceptron (MLP) student model.  Key aspects include the use of a teacher-bounded regression loss and the Information Bottleneck principle to ensure the distilled knowledge is relevant and transferable.  Spatial and temporal correlations are explicitly encoded to improve generalization.  The authors demonstrate that FlowDistill outperforms state-of-the-art models in terms of prediction accuracy, while requiring significantly less training data and having lower memory usage and inference latency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using knowledge distillation to transfer traffic flow prediction capabilities from an LLM to a lightweight MLP has some novelty.  While prior works have explored KD in traffic prediction (e.g., ST-MLP, EasyST), they typically use graph-based models as teachers. Exploring LLMs as teachers is a reasonably innovative step.  The specific combination of the teacher-bounded loss, information bottleneck principle, and the explicit modeling of spatial and temporal correlations adds to the novelty, although each of these components has been explored in other contexts.

*   **Significance:** Traffic prediction is a crucial task with numerous practical applications.  The challenge of limited training data is also very relevant in many real-world scenarios. The proposed FlowDistill framework appears promising in addressing these challenges, offering both improved performance and reduced computational cost, making it deployable in resource-constrained environments. If validated, this would have a direct positive impact.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Data Efficiency:** The most compelling strength of the paper is its data efficiency. The results consistently show FlowDistill achieving comparable or better accuracy than state-of-the-art methods with significantly less training data.
    *   **Computational Efficiency:** The use of a lightweight MLP and knowledge distillation results in lower memory usage and faster inference times compared to complex graph-based models.
    *   **Clear Problem Statement and Solution:** The paper clearly articulates the problem and presents a well-defined solution with a detailed explanation of each component.
    *   **Comprehensive Experimental Evaluation:** The authors conduct extensive experiments on two real-world datasets, comparing FlowDistill against several strong baselines and exploring the impact of various hyperparameters. The ablation study helps understand the contribution of each module.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependency on Fine-Tuned LLM:** While FlowDistill reduces the operational cost, it inherits a dependence on a fine-tuned LLM, namely, UrbanGPT. Fine-tuning an LLM (even once) is computationally expensive and requires substantial data. The cost of this one-time fine-tuning is not explicitly factored into the claimed efficiency gains of the end-to-end system. The authors need to clarify the resources and time investment required in fine-tuning the LLM, as this could reduce the attractiveness of FlowDistill for low-resource settings. This initial fine-tuning cost and the impact of the quality of fine-tuning on the overall KD process were not well-discussed.
    *   **Limited Architectural Exploration:** The student model is a simple MLP. While this contributes to computational efficiency, there might be other lightweight architectures that could further enhance performance, especially in capturing spatial-temporal dynamics. It could be valuable to explore more advanced models, such as temporal convolutional networks, in order to better capture these dynamics.
    *   **Generalizability of LLM:** The LLM fine-tuned on, say, NYC data may not be directly transferable to other cities with different traffic patterns and road network characteristics. The paper should discuss how the knowledge learned by the teacher LLM can be generalized to different cities and discuss the potential need for additional fine-tuning when adapting the framework to new urban environments.
*   **Potential Influence:** The potential for FlowDistill to enable accurate and efficient traffic prediction in resource-constrained settings or data-scarce regions is significant. It could lead to improved traffic management, reduced congestion, and optimized mobility services. It may also encourage further research into knowledge distillation strategies involving LLMs for spatio-temporal tasks.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a novel and significant contribution to traffic prediction by proposing a method to transfer knowledge from LLMs to computationally efficient MLPs. By addressing the challenges of data scarcity and computational cost, FlowDistill fills a gap in the existing research landscape and opens up new possibilities for real-world deployments. However, the dependency on a previously fine-tuned LLM, the limited architectural exploration of the student model, and a lack of discussion on the generalizability of the pre-trained LLM reduce the overall score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### ContrastScore: Towards Higher Quality, Less Biased, More Efficient Evaluation Metrics with Contrastive Evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02106v1)
- **Authors**: Xiao Wang, Daniil Larionov, Siwei Wu, Yiqi Liu, Steffen Eger, Nafise Sadat Moosavi, Chenghua Lin
- **Abstract**: Evaluating the quality of generated text automatically remains a significant challenge. Conventional reference-based metrics have been shown to exhibit relatively weak correlation with human evaluations. Recent research advocates the use of large language models (LLMs) as source-based metrics for natural language generation (NLG) assessment. While promising, LLM-based metrics, particularly those using smaller models, still fall short in aligning with human judgments. In this work, we introduce ContrastScore, a contrastive evaluation metric designed to enable higher-quality, less biased, and more efficient assessment of generated text. We evaluate ContrastScore on two NLG tasks: machine translation and summarization. Experimental results show that ContrastScore consistently achieves stronger correlation with human judgments than both single-model and ensemble-based baselines. Notably, ContrastScore based on Qwen 3B and 0.5B even outperforms Qwen 7B, despite having only half as many parameters, demonstrating its efficiency. Furthermore, it effectively mitigates common evaluation biases such as length and likelihood preferences, resulting in more robust automatic evaluation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ContrastScore: Towards Higher Quality, Less Biased, More Efficient Evaluation Metrics with Contrastive Evaluation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ContrastScore, a new evaluation metric for text generation that leverages the disagreement between two language models of differing capacities. Instead of relying on a single model's likelihood estimates (like BARTScore), ContrastScore uses a smaller, auxiliary model as a contrastive signal. It does this by measuring the absolute difference between the probabilities assigned by a stronger ("expert") and a weaker ("amateur") model. The authors evaluate ContrastScore on machine translation and summarization tasks, demonstrating that it achieves higher correlations with human judgments than existing metrics (single-model and ensemble-based) and mitigates biases like length and likelihood preferences.  The authors also show that ContrastScore is more computationally efficient, offering speed improvements compared to using larger single models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using a contrastive approach for *evaluation* is reasonably novel. While contrastive decoding is a known technique in *generation*, applying similar principles to evaluation provides a new perspective. The novelty is further strengthened by the specific formulation, which uses the absolute difference between probabilities and a scaling factor to control the influence of the amateur model.  While the core idea has roots in contrastive learning, the application to automatic evaluation is a valuable distinction.

*   **Significance:**  The significance of the work stems from several factors:

    *   **Improved Correlation:**  Demonstrating higher correlation with human judgments is a significant step towards more reliable automatic evaluation. The consistently better performance across multiple tasks and datasets strengthens this claim. The efficiency of contrastscore is an additional advantage, allowing for quicker and more reliable evaluation.
    *   **Bias Mitigation:**  Addressing the biases of LLM-based metrics (length and likelihood) is crucial. Showing that ContrastScore reduces these biases makes it a more robust and trustworthy evaluation method.
    *   **Efficiency:**  The computational efficiency is also significant. Reducing the computational demands allows for the application to larger datasets and can democratize access to high-quality evaluation.
    *   **Model selection:** The model selection framework is simple and does not provide sufficient details of how the models were trained and optimized.
    *   **Scope of Evaluation:** The paper could benefit from broader application on different language models as well as applications for specialized domains.
    *   **Limited Exploration of hyperparameters:** The setting of hyperparameters such as scaling factor and model temperatures need better support and more thorough investigation.

**Strengths:**

*   Clear and well-written paper.
*   Solid experimental setup across multiple tasks and datasets.
*   Demonstrated improvements in correlation, bias mitigation, and efficiency.
*   Ablation studies showing the contribution of the contrastive signal.

**Weaknesses:**

*   Lack of broader explanation for contrast score working for some tasks and not others.
*   Limited hyperparameter tuning.
*   A possible limitation is that the choice of expert and amateur models requires careful consideration. A poorly chosen pair could potentially degrade performance. The framework could be more robust with some criteria for selecting the base and auxiliary models.

**Justification of Score:**

Overall, the paper makes a solid contribution to the field of automatic evaluation. It provides a novel approach, demonstrates significant improvements over existing methods, and addresses crucial limitations of current LLM-based metrics. While the method has room for future refinement and expansion, the core idea is valuable and well-executed.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### TiC-LM: A Web-Scale Benchmark for Time-Continual LLM Pretraining
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02107v1)
- **Authors**: Jeffrey Li, Mohammadreza Armandpour, Iman Mirzadeh, Sachin Mehta, Vaishaal Shankar, Raviteja Vemulapalli, Samy Bengio, Oncel Tuzel, Mehrdad Farajtabar, Hadi Pouransari, Fartash Faghri
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) trained on historical web data inevitably become outdated. We investigate evaluation strategies and update methods for LLMs as new data becomes available. We introduce a web-scale dataset for time-continual pretraining of LLMs derived from 114 dumps of Common Crawl (CC) - orders of magnitude larger than previous continual language modeling benchmarks. We also design time-stratified evaluations across both general CC data and specific domains (Wikipedia, StackExchange, and code documentation) to assess how well various continual learning methods adapt to new data while retaining past knowledge. Our findings demonstrate that, on general CC data, autoregressive meta-schedules combined with a fixed-ratio replay of older data can achieve comparable held-out loss to re-training from scratch, while requiring significantly less computation (2.6x). However, the optimal balance between incorporating new data and replaying old data differs as replay is crucial to avoid forgetting on generic web data but less so on specific domains.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "TiC-LM: A Web-Scale Benchmark for Time-Continual LLM Pretraining":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TiC-LM, a large-scale benchmark designed for time-continual pretraining of Large Language Models (LLMs).  The benchmark uses 114 monthly dumps of Common Crawl (CC) data, totaling 2.9T tokens, which is significantly larger than existing continual learning benchmarks.  TiC-LM includes time-stratified evaluations across general CC data and domain-specific datasets (Wikipedia, StackExchange, code documentation) to assess adaptation and knowledge retention. The authors evaluate various continual learning methods (optimization-based, replay-based, regularization-based) and compare them to periodic retraining from scratch (Oracle).  Key findings show that autoregressive meta-schedules combined with replay can approach the performance of retraining from scratch with less computation, but the optimal balance of replay differs across data types.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary strength lies in the scale and time-stratification of the TiC-LM benchmark.  While continual learning of LLMs isn't entirely new, existing benchmarks are significantly smaller in terms of data size and the number of timesteps considered. The move to 114 months of Common Crawl, combined with domain-specific evaluations, is a valuable contribution. The emphasis on temporal distribution shifts in web-scale data makes the benchmark more realistic for real-world LLM deployment scenarios. That said, the *methods* explored (optimization-based, replay, regularization) are all well-established in the CL literature. The novelty is primarily in the application of these methods to a much larger and temporally-aware dataset and benchmark, and in the careful empirical analysis of their trade-offs. The inclusion of domain-specific evaluations (Wikipedia, StackExchange, and Code) is also a strong point as it goes beyond a generic web-crawl evaluation and adds more value.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses an increasingly important practical challenge: how to update LLMs cost-effectively as new data becomes available, overcoming the limitations of knowledge cutoffs. The findings about the effectiveness of replay combined with autoregressive meta-schedules are valuable, providing a tangible approach for continual learning. The observation that the optimal replay strategy varies depending on the data domain (generic web data vs. specific domains like StackOverflow) is an important nuance.  The release of the TiC-LM benchmark itself is a major contribution, providing a valuable resource for the research community to develop and evaluate more effective continual learning algorithms for LLMs. A key takeaway is that while continual learning is often presented as a universal solution, its effectiveness heavily depends on the characteristics of the data stream.  The analysis helps clarify when it's beneficial and when more frequent retraining from scratch might be preferable.

*   **Weaknesses:** While the empirical analysis is thorough, the paper could benefit from more theoretical insights.  For instance, understanding *why* the optimal replay ratio varies across domains could lead to more robust and adaptive continual learning methods.  Additionally, the paper focuses primarily on pretraining.  The paper acknowledges limitations such as only focusing on perplexity-based metrics (which can be limited). The findings may not directly translate to other downstream tasks or instruction-tuned LLMs without further research. The ethical considerations section is relatively brief and could be expanded to address potential biases inherent in the Common Crawl data, and the influence of temporal dynamics on these biases. The use of only H100s also restricts accessibility, although publishing model checkpoints would alleviate this.

*   **Impact:** The paper is likely to have a moderate to strong impact on the field.  The benchmark will serve as a valuable tool for researchers. The empirical findings, while not radically novel in terms of method, provide valuable insights into the trade-offs of different continual learning approaches in realistic web-scale LLM settings.

*   **Score Justification:** I assign a score of 7. The scale and realism of the TiC-LM benchmark, and the solid empirical analysis, make a valuable contribution to the field of continual learning for LLMs. However, the lack of more novel algorithmic ideas and the focus solely on pretraining limits the paper's overall significance.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### ScreenAudit: Detecting Screen Reader Accessibility Errors in Mobile Apps Using Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02110v1)
- **Authors**: Mingyuan Zhong, Ruolin Chen, Xia Chen, James Fogarty, Jacob O. Wobbrock
- **Abstract**: Many mobile apps are inaccessible, thereby excluding people from their potential benefits. Existing rule-based accessibility checkers aim to mitigate these failures by identifying errors early during development but are constrained in the types of errors they can detect. We present ScreenAudit, an LLM-powered system designed to traverse mobile app screens, extract metadata and transcripts, and identify screen reader accessibility errors overlooked by existing checkers. We recruited six accessibility experts including one screen reader user to evaluate ScreenAudit's reports across 14 unique app screens. Our findings indicate that ScreenAudit achieves an average coverage of 69.2%, compared to only 31.3% with a widely-used accessibility checker. Expert feedback indicated that ScreenAudit delivered higher-quality feedback and addressed more aspects of screen reader accessibility compared to existing checkers, and that ScreenAudit would benefit app developers in real-world settings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ScreenAudit, an LLM-powered system designed to detect screen reader accessibility errors in mobile apps. The system automates TalkBack (Android's default screen reader) to traverse app screens, capture metadata (including TalkBack transcripts), and uses OpenAI's GPT-4 to identify accessibility issues. It aims to address the limitations of existing rule-based accessibility checkers, which have limited error coverage. The authors conducted an expert study involving six accessibility experts (including a screen reader user) to evaluate ScreenAudit's reports across 14 app screens. The results demonstrate that ScreenAudit achieves a significantly higher coverage of accessibility errors compared to a widely-used accessibility checker, and experts find it to provide higher-quality feedback and address more aspects of screen reader accessibility. The paper also explores different prompting strategies for the LLM and their impact on error detection.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in combining LLMs with automated screen reader traversal and transcript analysis to detect accessibility errors. While LLMs have been used for UI understanding and automated UI testing, their application to *analyzing screen reader accessibility directly* and generating actionable feedback is relatively novel. This is an advancement over the traditional approach of rule-based checkers or static analysis. The method of augmenting screen reader outputs by direct integration of LLMs to audit accessibility is a contribution to the field.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant problem: the lack of accessibility in mobile apps and the limited effectiveness of current accessibility checkers. The findings suggest that LLMs can play a crucial role in improving the quality and coverage of accessibility audits, potentially leading to more inclusive apps. This is especially important given the increasing reliance on mobile devices for accessing information and services.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   *Empirical Validation:* The expert study provides strong evidence of ScreenAudit's effectiveness. The high error coverage and positive expert feedback add credibility to the approach.
    *   *Actionable Feedback:* The system generates reports with explanations and suggested actions, which are valuable for developers.
    *   *Addresses a Real-World Problem:* The paper focuses on a practical issue with a clear societal impact.
    *   *Prompt Engineering Exploration:* The analysis of different prompting strategies provides valuable insights into how to best leverage LLMs for accessibility assessment.
    *   *Clear System Description:* The paper clearly describes the system's architecture and implementation details.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   *Limited Scope:* The system currently focuses only on screen reader accessibility and primarily addresses static analysis of the existing UI. The integration with the app's dynamic features or accessibility metadata requires further investigation.
    *   *LLM Dependency:* The performance of the system relies heavily on the capabilities of the LLM (GPT-4). The risk of inaccurate results or lack of context requires mitigation. The sensitivity of the results to the specific LLM used and prompt design requires additional research on the reliability of the system.
    *   *Limited UI Element Coverage*: The reliance on TalkBack's core pipeline means that only elements reachable by screen readers are analyzed, limiting the system's detection of all potential accessibility issues.
    *   *Hallucinations*: While the paper briefly acknowledged the LLM is subject to hallucinations, more extensive mitigations strategies and empirical validation is needed.

*   **Potential Influence:** ScreenAudit has the potential to influence the development of future accessibility tools and practices. It could inspire the integration of LLMs into existing accessibility checkers, leading to more comprehensive and user-friendly solutions. The exploration of prompt engineering techniques can also guide future research in this area. The work demonstrates how LLMs may be used to simulate diverse user abilities and interactions.

*   **Justification:** While LLMs are rapidly advancing the field of accessibility, it is important to proceed with caution as the system has many limitations, depending on the abilities of the LLM, which can suffer from hallucinations. The authors conduct a formative study that shows that they are building on top of the knowledge needed by current app developers. The results presented from the expert study are promising, but more needs to be done before the system is fully functional.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel approach with significant potential impact. The strong empirical results and actionable feedback generation are key strengths. However, the limitations related to scope, UI coverage, LLM dependency, hallucination, and the lack of interactional elements need to be addressed in future work. The study's reliance on a limited set of expert-selected apps and elements restricts the generalizability of the coverage and precision metrics. The paper makes a solid contribution, but more research is needed to fully realize the potential of LLMs in accessibility assessment. A score of 7 reflects the paper's novelty, empirical support, and significance, balanced by its limitations and the need for further development and validation.

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 7/10

### Exploring LLM Reasoning Through Controlled Prompt Variations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02111v1)
- **Authors**: Giannis Chatziveroglou, Richard Yun, Maura Kelleher
- **Abstract**: This study investigates the reasoning robustness of large language models (LLMs) on mathematical problem-solving tasks under systematically introduced input perturbations. Using the GSM8K dataset as a controlled testbed, we evaluate how well state-of-the-art models maintain logical consistency and correctness when confronted with four categories of prompt perturbations: irrelevant context, pathological instructions, factually relevant but non-essential context, and a combination of the latter two. Our experiments, conducted on thirteen open-source and closed-source LLMs, reveal that introducing irrelevant context within the model's context window significantly degrades performance, suggesting that distinguishing essential from extraneous details remains a pressing challenge. Surprisingly, performance regressions are relatively insensitive to the complexity of the reasoning task, as measured by the number of steps required, and are not strictly correlated with model size. Moreover, we observe that certain perturbations inadvertently trigger chain-of-thought-like reasoning behaviors, even without explicit prompting. Our findings highlight critical vulnerabilities in current LLMs and underscore the need for improved robustness against noisy, misleading, and contextually dense inputs, paving the way for more resilient and reliable reasoning in real-world applications.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the paper and will provide a summary, followed by a critical evaluation and a novelty/significance score.

**Summary:**

This paper explores the reasoning robustness of Large Language Models (LLMs) on mathematical problem-solving tasks, specifically using the GSM8K dataset. The authors systematically introduce four categories of prompt perturbations: irrelevant context, pathological instructions, factually relevant but non-essential context, and a combination of the latter two. They then evaluate the performance of thirteen open-source and closed-source LLMs under these perturbations, comparing it to their baseline performance on the original, unperturbed prompts. The key findings are: 1) irrelevant context significantly degrades performance, highlighting the difficulty LLMs have in distinguishing essential from extraneous information; 2) performance regressions are surprisingly insensitive to the complexity of the reasoning task (number of steps); and 3) model size doesn't strictly correlate with robustness.  The authors also observe instances where certain perturbations inadvertently trigger chain-of-thought-like reasoning. The paper concludes that current LLMs have vulnerabilities and that improved robustness against noisy and misleading inputs is crucial for real-world applications.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   *Systematic Perturbation Study:* The paper's strength lies in its systematic approach to evaluating LLM robustness.  The four categories of prompt perturbations are well-defined and relevant to real-world scenarios where inputs are rarely clean.
    *   *Controlled Experiment:* Using GSM8K as a controlled testbed allows for a relatively objective comparison of model performance across different perturbations.
    *   *Variety of Models Evaluated:*  Evaluating a range of open-source and closed-source LLMs (though access to full context length was limited) increases the generalizability of the findings.
    *   *Interesting Observations:* The finding that irrelevant context is the most detrimental and the observation of accidental chain-of-thought-like behavior are noteworthy and warrant further investigation.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   *Limited Scale:* While the experimental design is sound, the sample size of 56 data points per experiment is relatively small. While the authors describe the sampling process in detail, it's unclear whether this sample truly captures the diversity and difficulty of the full GSM8K test set. Repeating the experiments with a larger sample would increase statistical significance. The number of data points are more impactful for some of the open-source models which were run a single time due to the aforementioned constriants.
    *   *Surface-Level Analysis:*  While the paper identifies vulnerabilities, it doesn't delve deeply into *why* these vulnerabilities exist. The discussion remains largely descriptive, lacking a mechanistic explanation of the models' reasoning failures. What part of the model's architecture is driving the death spiral? What triggers chain-of-thought-like response? Additional experiments to pinpoint the causes of the observed behaviour would strengthen the paper significantly.
    *   *Irrelevant Context generation:* The description of the irrelevant context generation could be more detailed. For example how the ratio of number of wikipedia articles, financial statements, and media articles was determined and what method was used to prevent data leakage. Also, how was the context length split between the added information and the original question.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The paper builds upon existing work on LLM robustness and sensitivity to context. Specifically, previous works GSM-symbolic, GSM+, and distracted context. However, it extends these studies by evaluating a wider range of models, including recent ones, and by introducing a novel combination of perturbation types.
    *   While the findings are not entirely surprising (i.e., LLMs struggle with noisy inputs), the systematic quantification of performance degradation across different perturbation categories provides valuable insights.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid, but not groundbreaking, contribution to the field. The systematic approach and evaluation of multiple models are valuable. However, the relatively small sample size and lack of in-depth analysis of underlying mechanisms limit its impact. A higher score would require more rigorous experimentation and a more comprehensive analysis of *why* these vulnerabilities exist. It's also important to consider that the field is rapidly evolving; robustness evaluations become more crucial as LLMs are deployed in more critical applications.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### PolyG: Effective and Efficient GraphRAG with Adaptive Graph Traversal
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02112v1)
- **Authors**: Renjie Liu, Haitian Jiang, Xiao Yan, Bo Tang, Jinyang Li
- **Abstract**: GraphRAG enhances large language models (LLMs) to generate quality answers for user questions by retrieving related facts from external knowledge graphs. Existing GraphRAG methods adopt a fixed graph traversal strategy for fact retrieval but we observe that user questions come in different types and require different graph traversal strategies. As such, existing GraphRAG methods are limited in effectiveness (i.e., quality of the generated answers) and/or efficiency (i.e., response time or the number of used tokens). In this paper, we propose to classify the questions according to a complete four-class taxonomy and adaptively select the appropriate graph traversal strategy for each type of questions. Our system PolyG is essentially a query planner for GraphRAG and can handle diverse questions with an unified interface and execution engine. Compared with SOTA GraphRAG methods, PolyG achieves an overall win rate of 75% on generation quality and a speedup up to 4x on response time.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "PolyG: Effective and Efficient GraphRAG with Adaptive Graph Traversal":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PolyG, a GraphRAG system designed to improve both the effectiveness and efficiency of question answering over knowledge graphs.  The key idea is to classify user questions into four distinct types based on the knowledge graph triple (subject, predicate, object) and adaptively select a suitable graph traversal strategy for each type. By using a query planner and unified execution engine, PolyG aims to address the limitations of existing GraphRAG methods that rely on fixed traversal strategies, which often struggle with diverse question types. The paper introduces a new GraphRAG benchmark with carefully crafted question templates to comprehensively evaluate different methods and metrics such as answer quality, end-to-end latency, and token usage. Experimental results demonstrate that PolyG consistently outperforms existing GraphRAG approaches, achieving higher answer quality and reducing response time and token consumption.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic approach to question classification and adaptive traversal strategy selection. While GraphRAG and question classification have been explored previously, the combination of a complete four-class question taxonomy tailored to the nature of knowledge graphs, alongside specific matching traversal strategies, is a unique contribution. The design of PolyG as a query planner for GraphRAG, enabling a unified interface and execution engine, also adds to the paper's novelty.

**Significance:**

*   **Improved Effectiveness:**  The core claim of improved answer quality (75% win rate) is significant. It suggests the adaptive approach is better at extracting relevant information from the knowledge graph for different question types.
*   **Enhanced Efficiency:**  The reported speedup (up to 4x) and reduction in token usage are practically significant for real-world deployment. Reducing LLM calls directly translates to cost savings and improved user experience.
*   **Generalizability:**  The taxonomy and adaptive strategy could be a useful framework for other researchers in the GraphRAG field, leading to more robust and efficient systems.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The new GraphRAG benchmark contributes to the field by providing a more diverse and challenging evaluation dataset than existing benchmarks, which often focus on a single question type.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-defined Problem:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing GraphRAG methods and provides a compelling motivation for the adaptive approach.
*   **Systematic Approach:** The four-class question taxonomy and corresponding traversal strategies are well-defined and intuitive.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:**  The experimental results consistently demonstrate the superiority of PolyG over baseline methods across various datasets and metrics.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** Metrics considered are not only accuracy but also efficiency (latency, token consumption).
*   **Clear Explanation:** The workflow of PolyG is well explained with examples and illustrations.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Taxonomy Exhaustiveness:** While the paper argues that the four question types cover a majority of graph-related queries, its inability to handle certain edge cases (namely global summaries) is a limitation. The work could be more robust by demonstrating a handling of more general cases.
*   **Reliance on LLMs:** The question classification relies on an LLM, which introduces a potential point of failure and adds to the overall complexity of the system. However, the authors make a sound argument in limiting it to a single call, where it consumes as little as 10% of the total time.
*   **Limited comparison:** While compared to several GraphRAG approaches, many of those only adopt a very naive method. Future works can compare against more recent and modern works and also test on larger and more challenging problems.
*   **Scalability:** It is unclear how well PolyG scales to very large knowledge graphs and complex queries. A more detailed analysis of scalability would be beneficial.
*   **Neo4j Dependency:** The reliance on Neo4j might limit the broader adoption of the system, especially for organizations using other graph databases.

**Potential Influence:** The paper presents a promising approach to GraphRAG that has the potential to influence the design of future question-answering systems over knowledge graphs. The ideas of adaptive traversal and explicit query planning could be adopted by other researchers and practitioners to develop more effective and efficient GraphRAG systems. However, its reliance on LLMs may limit its broader applications in specific spaces.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a solid contribution with a well-defined approach, compelling empirical evidence, and clear presentation. While limitations related to taxonomy completeness and reliance on LLMs exist, the core ideas are novel and significant. With further investigation into its scalability, it can have a higher impact in the field.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### LLMPi: Optimizing LLMs for High-Throughput on Raspberry Pi
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02118v1)
- **Authors**: Mahsa Ardakani, Jinendra Malekar, Ramtin Zand
- **Abstract**: Deploying Large Language Models (LLMs) on resource-constrained edge devices like the Raspberry Pi presents challenges in computational efficiency, power consumption, and response latency. This paper explores quantization-based optimization techniques to enable high-throughput, energy-efficient execution of LLMs on low-power embedded systems. Our approach leverages k-quantization, a Post-Training Quantization (PTQ) method designed for different bit-widths, enabling efficient 2-bit, 4-bit, 6-bit, and 8-bit weight quantization. Additionally, we employ ternary quantization using Quantization-Aware Training (QAT) for BitNet models, allowing for more effective adaptation to lower-bit representations while preserving accuracy. Our findings highlight the potential of quantized LLMs for real-time conversational AI on edge devices, paving the way for low-power, high-efficiency AI deployment in mobile and embedded applications. This study demonstrates that aggressive quantization strategies can significantly reduce energy consumption while maintaining inference quality, making LLMs practical for resource-limited environments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "LLMPi: Optimizing LLMs for High-Throughput on Raspberry Pi" explores quantization techniques to enable efficient deployment of Large Language Models (LLMs) on resource-constrained Raspberry Pi devices. It investigates k-quantization (Post-Training Quantization - PTQ) at different bit-widths (2-bit, 4-bit, 6-bit, and 8-bit) and ternary quantization (using Quantization-Aware Training - QAT) for BitNet models. The study evaluates these quantized models against FP16 baselines, measuring inference speed, energy efficiency, and response quality. The results highlight that aggressive quantization significantly reduces computational overhead while preserving acceptable inference quality, making LLM deployment feasible on resource-limited environments for applications like social robotics and mobile assistants.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies in its specific focus on the Raspberry Pi 5 and a systematic investigation of various quantization methods for modern LLMs (including newer models such as Gemma 2 and Llama 3) on this platform. While quantization and edge deployment of LLMs are not entirely new concepts, the combination of specific models, quantization techniques (k-quantization, ternary quantization with BitNet), and the target hardware (Raspberry Pi 5) provides a distinct contribution. The inclusion of detailed energy efficiency measurements is a positive aspect. However, several research areas have already tested similar theories. The paper doesn't introduce a wholly novel *methodology* for quantization or deployment; it's more of an empirical study.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the increasing demand for on-device AI. Enabling LLMs on resource-constrained edge devices like the Raspberry Pi addresses privacy concerns, reduces reliance on network connectivity, and lowers latency. The detailed performance analysis (throughput, energy efficiency, response quality) provides valuable insights for practitioners aiming to deploy LLMs in real-world embedded applications. The explicit definition of a "real-time" TPS threshold is useful.  The paper clearly articulates the trade-offs between quantization levels and model performance and offers a practical guide for selecting the appropriate quantization strategy based on application requirements. However, the lack of comparison against other edge devices limits the generalizability. Also, it would have been impactful to showcase a complete, end-to-end conversational AI application running on the Raspberry Pi using the optimized models.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Systematic Evaluation: Thorough evaluation of different quantization techniques and bit-widths.
    *   Practical Focus: Emphasis on real-time feasibility and energy efficiency, critical for edge deployment.
    *   Relevance: Addresses a growing need for on-device AI processing.
    *   Good benchmark study
    *   Use of relatively new LLMs in comparison

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited Methodology Innovation: No novel quantization algorithms are introduced.
    *   Hardware Limitation: Focus solely on Raspberry Pi 5 limits generalizability. Comparison with other edge devices would enhance the study.
    *   Lack of Real-World Application Showcase: The absence of a demonstration with a complete conversational AI system weakens the practical impact.
    *   NUBIA score is not sufficient to truly evaluate the quality of responses of the LLMs, this section could have been improved.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a valuable empirical study on the feasibility of deploying LLMs on Raspberry Pi using quantization techniques. While it lacks substantial methodological innovation in quantization algorithms (relying on existing methods like k-quantization), the comprehensive evaluation of different models and quantization levels on a specific, popular edge device (Raspberry Pi 5) is beneficial. The explicit focus on real-time performance and energy efficiency metrics enhances its practical value for researchers and practitioners in the field. However, the limited hardware scope and the lack of end-to-end application demonstration restrict its overall impact and novelty. A higher score would require either a novel quantization technique or a complete demonstration of a real-world application benefiting from the research.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Efficient Model Selection for Time Series Forecasting via LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02119v1)
- **Authors**: Wang Wei, Tiankai Yang, Hongjie Chen, Ryan A. Rossi, Yue Zhao, Franck Dernoncourt, Hoda Eldardiry
- **Abstract**: Model selection is a critical step in time series forecasting, traditionally requiring extensive performance evaluations across various datasets. Meta-learning approaches aim to automate this process, but they typically depend on pre-constructed performance matrices, which are costly to build. In this work, we propose to leverage Large Language Models (LLMs) as a lightweight alternative for model selection. Our method eliminates the need for explicit performance matrices by utilizing the inherent knowledge and reasoning capabilities of LLMs. Through extensive experiments with LLaMA, GPT and Gemini, we demonstrate that our approach outperforms traditional meta-learning techniques and heuristic baselines, while significantly reducing computational overhead. These findings underscore the potential of LLMs in efficient model selection for time series forecasting.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach to time series forecasting model selection by leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs).  Instead of relying on traditional meta-learning methods that require computationally expensive performance matrices, the authors propose using LLMs with zero-shot prompting to infer the best forecasting model directly. The paper explores the effectiveness of different LLMs (Llama, GPT, and Gemini) with varying prompt designs, demonstrating that LLM-based selection outperforms both popular forecasting models and meta-learning approaches.  The approach also offers significant computational efficiency by eliminating the need for extensive training and evaluation of multiple models. An ablation study investigates the impact of incorporating meta-features and chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning in prompts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The primary novelty lies in the application of LLMs to the *model selection* problem in time series forecasting, specifically without relying on pre-computed performance matrices. Previous works have explored LLMs *as forecasting models* themselves, but this paper tackles the distinct problem of *choosing* the best model. The zero-shot aspect further adds to the novelty, eliminating the need for task-specific exemplars or fine-tuning. However, the use of prompting is becoming a standard practice with LLMs; therefore, it is the particular application here that offers newness.

* **Significance:**  The potential significance is substantial. Model selection is a crucial and often time-consuming step in time series forecasting.  The ability to streamline this process using readily available LLMs with minimal computational overhead has practical implications. The reported reduction in inference time compared to exhaustive evaluation or even matrix-based meta-learning is valuable.
